\section{Results, quantifiers, and source boundary}
\label{fup:eh:sec:scope}
The two circuits in this report are distinct. The
\emph{auxiliary square/product82} candidate frees the supplied products
$f^2$ and $Tf$ while retaining the paid auxiliary coefficient
$S=i\D c^2$. The \emph{free-coefficient83} candidate instead retains
$f$ and $T$ and supplies an independent positive coefficient root $S$.
Neither is Report41's first-index-deleted82 candidate.

Report45 proves that every genuine inherited compiler slice of the
square/product82 candidate accepts every positive ordinary input by an
explicit strictly positive 18-witness counterfamily \cite{fup:eh:report45}.
We use exactly that construction. The fixed six numerals and ordinary
input are never changed. Its all-input existence theorem is an imported
proved result; the quantitative and nonextension results below are proved
here in full. The recovered Report45 proof and all original source bytes
are supplied as inert data, with exact identities in Appendix~\ref{fup:eh:app:pins}.

For a positive integer $z$, let $\bl(z)=\lfloor\log_2z\rfloor+1$.
The \emph{height} of a supplied witness tuple is its numerical maximum;
intermediate circuit values such as $S,V,c$ are not extra supplied
witnesses. Write $y$ for the exponent called \code{yexp} in the
construction, and reserve $\yaux$ for its auxiliary Pell witness.

\begin{theorem}[Exact height of the displayed completion]
\label{fup:eh:thm:height}
Fix a genuine compiler, a positive ordinary input, and one Report45
outer tuple. Set
\[
 L=p+y+1,\qquad C=(p-1)L,\qquad
 T_0=2pL-1,\qquad N=(R-1)T_0.
\]
For the displayed completion $i=1$, the largest of all 18 supplied
coordinates is strictly $\yaux=\ps{\D c^2}{R}$. In particular, if
$\mathcal H$ denotes its height, then
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:exactheight}
 2^N<\mathcal H=\yaux<2^{N+1},\qquad \bl(\mathcal H)=N+1.
\end{equation}
The exact branch polynomials for $\bl(\mathcal H)$ are
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:cubics}
\begin{array}{ll}
 \mathrm A,\ \epsilon=+1:&(4R^3+4R^2-7R+2)/3,\\
 \mathrm A,\ \epsilon=-1:&(4R^3-12R^2+9R+2)/3,\\
 \mathrm B:&(25R^3+25R^2-39R+3)/14.
\end{array}
\end{equation}
The 18-row ledger in Section~\ref{fup:eh:sec:ledger} has 11 exact bit lengths
and seven certified upper bounds; it does not assert 18 exact lengths.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Unique minimum with the outer tuple fixed]
\label{fup:eh:thm:min}
Fix the same 14 non-auxiliary supplied coordinates. Among every
positive integer choice of $(i,\Fa,\Ua,\yaux)$ making the complete
square/product82 polynomial zero, the displayed completion is the
unique minimizer of the maximum of all 18 supplied coordinates.
Its minimum is exactly $\ps{\D c^2}{R}$ and has
\eqref{fup:eh:eq:exactheight}--\eqref{fup:eh:eq:cubics}. It also simultaneously
minimizes the four auxiliary coordinates; $\yaux$ and $\Ua$ are uniquely
minimized at this completion, while $\Fa$ alone is minimal for every
admissible Pell index when $i=1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Even-rank nonextension to free83]
\label{fup:eh:thm:free}
Let $A\ge4$ be even, $\D=A^2-1$, $p\ge2$ even,
$c=\ps A p$, and $R$ odd. Retain five outer factors with product
$P_5=1$. There are no positive integers $f,S,T,y_*$ satisfying
\begin{align}
 V&=c(Tf-1)-Rf^2,\label{fup:eh:eq:freeV}\\
 \Na&=S^2V^2-(S^2-1)y_*^2,\qquad
 \Ns=\D f^2-S^2,\label{fup:eh:eq:freenorms}\\
 \Na\Ns&=\D.\label{fup:eh:eq:freeproduct}
\end{align}
There is no sign restriction on the intermediate integer $V$ and no
squarefreeness assumption on $\D$. Thus no Report45 outer tuple can
be transferred to free83 merely by changing these four auxiliaries.
\end{theorem}

The first two results are exact within each fixed outer tuple. They
give no lower bound for certificates from a different outer construction.
The third excludes one broad invariant sector of free83.
During final preparation, a separate commit-pinned construction was
independently audited: the unchanged inherited free83 compiler recipe
has infinitely many full positive zeros at every positive ordinary
input on every valid fixed-program slice, using prime main indices
$p\equiv1\pmod4$ \cite{fup:eh:primecollapse}. That all-input collapse is
compatible with our nonextension theorem for fixed even main indices.
It excludes neither other 83-operation circuits nor different
coefficient recipes. No new universal operation bound, generic
soundness theorem, or priority claim is made here.

\section{The fixed counterfamily and the literal interfaces}
\label{fup:eh:sec:setup}
We record the construction contract sufficiently explicitly to identify
every coordinate used below. The full Chinese-remainder existence and
positivity proof is in the supplied Report45 packet \cite{fup:eh:report45,fup:eh:base}.
Our notation deliberately separates the height exponent $C$ from the
packing expression $C_{\mathrm{pack}}$.

\subsection{Compiler and packing data}
The six genuine fixed ports are $B-1,K,\ell,b,M_C,M_F$, where
\[
 B=2^d,\quad d=5^a,\quad a\ge1,\quad \ell=2d,\quad K>0,
\]
$b\ge5$ is an odd power of five, and the inherited masks satisfy
$M_F=M_{F,0}+B-1$, $0<M_C,M_{F,0}<B-1$, $M_C\equiv2\pmod4$.
These are consequences of a genuine compiler, not a replacement
definition by necessary numerical conditions.

For $x\in\pos$, set $I=\ell x+b$, $W=2^I$, and choose
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:radix}
 M_*=\max\{I,\bl(K),61\},\quad
 r=\max\{a,\lceil\log_5(4M_*)\rceil\},\quad t=5^r,\quad q=2^t.
\end{equation}
Larger $r$ also give family members; \eqref{fup:eh:eq:radix} is the least
admissible choice for the global input bounds. All height and minimality
statements hold for every allowed $r$. Define
\[
 J=\frac{q-1}{B-1},\quad Q=q^2-1,\quad
 M_{\mathrm{mask}}=(M_C+qM_F)J,\quad m_3=(M_C+2M_F)\bmod3.
\]
The packing parameters $e,Z,\epsilon$ are those of Report45, with
$1\le e\le60$, $1\le Z\le6t$, and
\begin{align}
 F&=(K+2^e)(W+Z)+1-\epsilon,\label{fup:eh:eq:pack}\\
 \alpha&=q-F-2Z-W-\ell x,\nonumber\\
 R&=(q^2-qF-Z)(q^2-1)+M_{\mathrm{mask}}.\nonumber
\end{align}
The residue-controlled branch parameters are
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:branches}
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c|c}
\text{branch}&R&p&n&y\\\hline
\mathrm A\ (m_3\in\{1,2\})&6u-\epsilon&4u&3u&2u-1\\
\mathrm B\ (m_3=0)&28v-1&20v&14v&15v-1
\end{array}
\end{equation}
Here $\epsilon=+1$ if $m_3=2$, $\epsilon=-1$ if $m_3=1$,
and $\epsilon=+1$ in Branch B. The CRT choice ensures
$p\equiv e\pmod t$; it uses the actual packed $R$ above.
The inherited proof supplies
\begin{gather}
 t\ge625,\quad 0<J,\alpha<q,\quad
 F\le 2^{t/2+2}+2,\quad W+Z<2^{t/4+1},\label{fup:eh:eq:outerbounds}\\
 2^{3t}<R<q^4-q^3,\quad R\equiv3\pmod4,\quad
 p\equiv0\pmod4,\quad p>I,\quad p>56t,\quad y\ge3t.\label{fup:eh:eq:rankbounds}
\end{gather}
In particular $I$ is odd, $p\ge16$, $y\ge3$, $n\ge3$, $R\ge8$,
and $R<2p$ directly from \eqref{fup:eh:eq:branches}.

\subsection{All witness definitions and retained factors}
For $A\ge2$, our Pell notation is
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:pell}
 (A+\sqrt{A^2-1})^j=\ch A j+\ps A j\sqrt{A^2-1}.
\end{equation}
Both sequences obey $z_{j+2}=2Az_{j+1}-z_j$ with initial pairs
$(\chi_0,\chi_1)=(1,A)$, $(\psi_0,\psi_1)=(0,1)$.
Set
\begin{gather*}
 w=2^{p-t},\quad s=2^{y-3t},\quad X=wq=2^p,\quad Y=sq^3=2^y,\quad E=XY,\\
 a_0=Y(X+1),\quad A=a_0+2,\quad \D=A^2-1,\quad H_0=4a_0+3,\quad P=2XY^2+1,\\
 D=\ch A p,\quad c=\ps A p,\quad \tau=\ch P n,\quad k=2\ps P n,\\
 \eta=c-kY,\quad \zeta=k-\eta,\quad h=(k-2n)/E,\\
 g_j=\frac{\ch A j-a_0\ps A j-2^j}{H_0},\quad
 \rho=g_I,\quad \sigma=g_p-g_I,\quad \delta=\frac{\ps A I-I}{\D},\\
 \ttt=1+\frac{(W+Z)(w-2^e)}{q-1}.
\end{gather*}
These expressions define integers by the proved construction.
The ratio estimate needed later is
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:ratio}
 0<\frac c k-Y<\frac{4pY}{X}<1.
\end{equation}
Let $\kappa=\ps A I$, $\mu=\ch A I$,
$C_{\mathrm{pack}}=q-F-Z-\alpha-\ell x=W+Z$. The five retained factors are
\begin{align*}
 N_{\mathrm{first}}&=\tau^2-XY^2(XY^2+1)k^2=1,\\
 N_{\mathrm{main}}&=D^2-\D c^2=1,\qquad
 N_{\mathrm{input}}=\mu^2-\D\kappa^2=1,\\
 N_{\mathrm{index}}&=k-hE-R=\epsilon,\\
 N_{\mathrm{transport}}&=(K+w)C_{\mathrm{pack}}+q-F-\ttt(q-1)=\epsilon.
\end{align*}
Their product is $P_5=1$, including the branch with two negative factors.
The shared projections remain literally
$\kappa=I+\delta\D$, $\mu=W+a_0\kappa+\rho H_0$, and
$D=X+a_0c+(\rho+\sigma)H_0$.

\subsection{The square/product82 auxiliary block}
The exact polynomial identity is
\begin{align}
 S&=i\D c^2,\qquad V=c(\Ua-1)-R\Fa,\label{fup:eh:eq:82aux}\\
 \Na&=S^2V^2-(S^2-1)\yaux^2,\qquad Q_s=\Fa-\D i^2c^4,\nonumber\\
 P_{82}&=\D(P_5\Na Q_s-1).\label{fup:eh:eq:82poly}
\end{align}
In particular the strong factor is $\D\Fa-S^2=\D Q_s$;
we have not dropped the paid finalizer or substituted separate equations
for the polynomial. The displayed completion is
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:completion}
 i=1,\quad S_0=\D c^2,\quad \Fa=\D c^4+1,\quad
 V=\frac{\ch{S_0}R}{S_0},\quad \yaux=\ps{S_0}R,\quad
 \Ua=\frac{V+R\Fa}{c}+1.
\end{equation}
Its source-ordered 18 supplied coordinates are
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:witnesslist}
 J,F,\alpha,\ttt,\Fa,h,i,\Ua,s,w,
 \tau,\eta,\zeta,\yaux,Z,\delta,\rho,\sigma.
\end{equation}
All are strictly positive. The four varying auxiliaries in
Theorem~\ref{fup:eh:thm:min} are precisely positions $5,7,8,14$.

\section{Pell estimates with an explicit error budget}
\label{fup:eh:sec:error}
For $a\ge2$ and $j\ge3$, recurrence and strict increase give
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:pellbounds}
 (2a-1)^{j-1}\le\ps a j<(2a)^{j-1}.
\end{equation}
Indeed $\psi_2=2a>2a-1$, and
$\psi_{j+1}>(2a-1)\psi_j$ for $j\ge2$ because
$\psi_j>\psi_{j-1}>0$. The upper recurrence inequality is strict once
the subtracted term is positive. Likewise
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:chibounds}
 a(2a-1)^{j-1}<\ch a j<a(2a)^{j-1}\qquad(j\ge2),
\end{equation}
since $\chi_2/\chi_1=2a-1/a>2a-1$ and all subsequent ratios
exceed $2a-1$ while remaining below $2a$.

Put
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:budgetvars}
 L=p+y+1,\quad C=(p-1)L,\quad F_0=4C+2L-2,\quad
 T_0=2pL-1,\quad N=(R-1)T_0.
\end{equation}
The exact branch resonance is equivalently
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:resonance}
 L_1=p+2y+2,\qquad (n-1)L_1=C-y-1,\qquad nL_1=pL.
\end{equation}
These follow by substitution in \eqref{fup:eh:eq:branches}; no approximate
exponent matching is used.

\begin{lemma}[Uniform perturbation bound]\label{fup:eh:lem:budget}
If $p\ge16$, $0\le z<2^{1-p}$, and $0\le j\le4p^2$ is integral,
then $1\le(1+z)^j<32/31$, except that the harmless value $1$ occurs
when $jz=0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The ratio of consecutive values of $p^2/2^p$ is
$(p+1)^2/(2p^2)<1$ for $p\ge3$. Thus
$jz<8p^2/2^p\le1/32$ at and above $p=16$.
Using $\binom jk\le j^k$, the binomial sum is at most the
geometric series $(1-jz)^{-1}<32/31$ when $jz>0$.
\end{proof}

Set $d_0=X^{-1}+2/(XY)$, so
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:Ad}
 2A=2^L(1+d_0),\quad 0<d_0<2^{1-p},\quad 2A-1>2^L.
\end{equation}
Every perturbation exponent below is at most $4p^2$; in particular
$2p(R-1)<4p^2$, $4p-2\le4p^2$, and $n<p$.
The norm parameter satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:Deltaest}
 2^{2L-2}<\D<2^{2L-2}(1+d_0)^2.
\end{equation}
The lower endpoint follows from
$(XY+Y+2)^2-1>(XY)^2$.

\section{Exact dominant bit lengths}
\label{fup:eh:sec:dominant}
For the displayed completion we henceforth write $S=S_0$.
Equations~\eqref{fup:eh:eq:pellbounds} and \eqref{fup:eh:eq:Ad} yield
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:cest}
 2^C<c<2^C(1+d_0)^{p-1}<(32/31)2^C.
\end{equation}
Combining with \eqref{fup:eh:eq:Deltaest},
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:Sest}
 2^{T_0-1}<S<2^{T_0-1}(1+d_0)^{2p}.
\end{equation}
Since $S$ is integral, $2S-1>2^{T_0}$. Applying the Pell bounds
at rank $R$ gives the strict dyadic enclosure
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:ybound}
 2^N<(2S-1)^{R-1}\le\yaux<(2S)^{R-1}
 <2^N(1+d_0)^{2p(R-1)}<(32/31)2^N<2^{N+1}.
\end{equation}
Thus $\bl(\yaux)=N+1$ exactly. Also
$V=\chi_S(R)/S>(2S-1)^{R-1}>2^N$, while its norm gives
\[
 V^2=(1-S^{-2})\yaux^2+S^{-2}<\yaux^2
\]
because $\yaux>1$. Hence $\bl(V)=N+1$ and $V<\yaux$.

The strong witness obeys
\[
 2^{F_0}<\Fa=\D c^4+1<(32/31)2^{F_0}+1<2^{F_0+1},
\]
where the combined perturbation exponent is $4p-2$ and $F_0\ge1$.
Consequently $\bl(\Fa)=F_0+1$.

For $\Ua$, division by $c$ must be controlled before adding the
remaining positive terms. Since
$2\D-1>2^{2L-1}$ and $2S-1>(2\D-1)c^2$,
\begin{align*}
 V/c&>(2\D-1)^{R-1}c^{2R-3}>2^{N-C},\\
 V/c&<(2\D)^{R-1}c^{2R-3}
 <2^{N-C}(1+d_0)^{2p(R-1)-p+1}<(32/31)2^{N-C}.
\end{align*}
The exponent identity
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:gap}
 N-F_0=(2R-6)C+(2R-4)L-R+3\ge R
\end{equation}
holds for $R\ge8$ and $C\ge15$ (already the $C$ term exceeds
$2R-3$). Because $F_0\ge C$, $c<2^{C+1}$, and
$R+1\le2^{R-2}$ for $R\ge8$, we obtain
\[
 R\Fa+c<(R+1)2^{F_0+1}\le2^{N-1}.
\]
Dividing by $c>2^C$ and adding to $V/c$ proves
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:Ubound}
 2^{N-C}<\Ua<(32/31+1/2)2^{N-C}<2^{N-C+1}.
\end{equation}
Thus $\bl(\Ua)=N-C+1$. In particular the additive terms do not
create a binary carry beyond this exact length.

\section{The complete 18-coordinate ledger}
\label{fup:eh:sec:ledger}
Let $\lambda=\lceil\log_2(W+Z)\rceil\le\lceil t/4\rceil+1$.
Here and below a real logarithm merely describes an exact integer
bound; a replay may compute $\lceil\log_2z\rceil$ as $\bl(z-1)$.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rll@{\qquad}l}
\toprule
Port&Coordinate&Relation&Bit length or upper bound\\
\midrule
1&$J$&$\le$&$t$\\
2&$F$&$\le$&$\lceil t/2\rceil+3$\\
3&$\alpha$&$\le$&$t$\\
4&$\ttt$&$\le$&$p-2t+\lambda+2$\\
5&$\Fa$&$=$&$4C+2L-1$\\
6&$h$&$=$&$C-p-2y+1$\\
7&$i$&$=$&$1$\\
8&$\Ua$&$=$&$N-C+1$\\
9&$s$&$=$&$y-3t+1$\\
10&$w$&$=$&$p-t+1$\\
11&$\tau$&$=$&$pL$\\
12&$\eta$&$\le$&$C-p+\lceil\log_2p\rceil+3$\\
13&$\zeta$&$\le$&$C-y+1$\\
14&$\yaux$&$=$&$N+1$\\
15&$Z$&$\le$&$\lfloor\log_2(6t)\rfloor+1$\\
16&$\delta$&$=$&$(I-3)L+3$\\
17&$\rho$&$=$&$(I-2)L+1$\\
18&$\sigma$&$=$&$(p-2)L+1$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\subsection{Packing, transport, and pure powers}
The $J,F,\alpha,Z$ bounds are immediate from
\eqref{fup:eh:eq:outerbounds}; $s,w,i$ have their exact lengths by definition.
For transport, use $q-1>2^{t-1}$ and $w-2^e<w$:
\[
 \ttt<1+2^{p-2t+\lambda+1}<2^{p-2t+\lambda+2}.
\]
The exponent is positive because $p>56t$. The table gives a
convenient integer upper bound, without claiming it is sharp.

\subsection{The first Pell block and ratio witnesses}
Set $M_0=2^{L_1}=4XY^2$ and $d_1=1/(2XY^2)$, so
$2P=M_0(1+d_1)$, $2P-1=M_0+1$, and $d_1<2^{1-p}$.
The resonance and Lemma~\ref{fup:eh:lem:budget} give
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:kest}
 2^{C-y}<k<(32/31)2^{C-y}<2^{C-y+1}.
\end{equation}
To justify subtracting $2n$ in $h$, a strict lower margin is needed:
\[
 \ps P n\ge(M_0+1)^{n-1}
 \ge M_0^{n-1}+(n-1)M_0^{n-2}>M_0^{n-1}+n,
\]
since $n\ge3$ and $M_0\ge4$. Hence
$k-2n>2M_0^{n-1}=2^{C-y}$. Dividing by $E=2^{L-1}$,
and using \eqref{fup:eh:eq:kest} for the upper endpoint, proves the exact
length of $h$.

From \eqref{fup:eh:eq:chibounds},
\[
 2^{nL_1-1}<\tau<2^{nL_1-1}(1+d_1)^n<2^{nL_1}=2^{pL}.
\]
Thus $\bl(\tau)=pL$ by \eqref{fup:eh:eq:resonance}. Finally
\eqref{fup:eh:eq:ratio} yields
\[
 0<\eta<4pkY/X<2^{C-p+3}p,\qquad 0<\zeta<k,
\]
which proves their two one-sided rows.

\subsection{The input-loader coordinates}
For odd $j$, put $d_j=(\psi_A(j)-j)/\D$. The step-two recurrence gives
\[
 d_1=0,\quad d_3=4,\quad
 d_{j+2}=(4A^2-2)d_j-d_{j-2}+4j.
\]
Induction gives $d_j>d_{j-2}\ge0$ for odd $j\ge3$ and
\[
 d_{j+2}>(4A^2-3)d_j>(2A-1)^2d_j.
\]
Consequently
\[
 2^{(I-3)L+2}\le d_I
 <\frac{\ps A I}{\D}
 <2^{(I-3)L+2}(1+d_0)^{I-1}<2^{(I-3)L+3}.
\]
The lower equality is allowed at $I=3$, where $\delta=d_3=4$.
This proves the exact $\delta$ row in all cases.

The sequence $g_j$ from Section~\ref{fup:eh:sec:setup} has
$g_0=g_1=0$, $g_2=1$, and
\[
 g_{j+1}=2Ag_j-g_{j-1}+2^{j-1}.
\]
Its strict increase implies
$g_j>(2A-1)^{j-2}$ for $j\ge3$.
For the upper bound, $g_j\ge2^{j-2}$ for $j\ge2$, so
$2^{j-1}\le2g_j$ and $g_{j+1}\le(2A+2)g_j$.
Therefore, for $3\le j\le p$,
\[
 (2A-1)^{j-2}<g_j\le(2A+2)^{j-2}.
\]
Since
$2A+2=2^L(1+X^{-1}+3/(XY))$ and the new perturbation is
less than $2^{1-p}$ when $Y\ge8$, the uniform budget yields
\[
 2^{(j-2)L}<g_j<(32/31)2^{(j-2)L}.
\]
This proves the $\rho=g_I$ row. Since $I\le p-1$,
\[
 \sigma\ge g_p-g_{p-1}>(2A-2)g_{p-1}>2^{(p-2)L},
 \qquad \sigma<g_p<2^{(p-2)L+1},
\]
where $2A-2>2^L$. This establishes its exact row without losing
the required strict lower endpoint to subtraction.

\subsection{Dominance and the exact cubic}
Every non-auxiliary row is at most $F_0+1$. For the two largest
inner bounds use $pL<4C+2L-1$ and
$\lceil\log_2p\rceil+3\le p$ for $p\ge16$; the other
comparisons follow from $I<p$, $y\ge3t$, $p>56t$, and
$\lambda\le\lceil t/4\rceil+1$.
Together with \eqref{fup:eh:eq:gap}, the exact lengths of $\Fa,\Ua,i$,
and \eqref{fup:eh:eq:ybound}, these comparisons show that $\yaux$ strictly
exceeds all other supplied coordinates.

In Branch A put $z=R+\epsilon$. Then $p=2z/3$, $L=z$, and
\[
 \bl(\mathcal H)=(R-1)\bigl(\tfrac43(R+\epsilon)^2-1\bigr)+1.
\]
In Branch B put $z=R+1$. Then $p=5z/7$, $L=5z/4$, and
\[
 \bl(\mathcal H)=(R-1)\bigl(\tfrac{25}{14}(R+1)^2-1\bigr)+1.
\]
Expansion proves \eqref{fup:eh:eq:cubics}, whose integrality is guaranteed by
the branch congruences. This completes Theorem~\ref{fup:eh:thm:height}.

\section{Radix dependence, global bounds, and genuine jumps}
\label{fup:eh:sec:global}
The least-radix formula \eqref{fup:eh:eq:radix} implies
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:radixenvelope}
 \max\{d,4M_*\}\le t\le\max\{d,20M_*\}.
\end{equation}
If $t>d$, minimality gives $t/5<4M_*$, so $t<20M_*$ strictly.
An integer-only implementation starts at $d=5^a$ and repeatedly
multiplies by five until reaching $4M_*$; no floating-point logarithm
is needed.

The packed index is much closer to $q^4$ than the coarse lower
bound $q^3$ suggests. Expanding \eqref{fup:eh:eq:pack},
\[
 R=q^4-q^3F-q^2(Z+1)+qF+Z+M_{\mathrm{mask}}.
\]
All three final summands are positive, and $R<q^4$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:theta}
 0<1-R/q^4< e_t:=4\,2^{-t/2}+2\,2^{-t}+(6t+1)2^{-2t}.
\end{equation}
For $t\ge625$, $6t+1<2^{t/2}$ and
$2\,2^{-t}+2^{-3t/2}<2^{-t/2}$. Thus
$e_t<5\,2^{-t/2}<1/100$.

It follows from the exact cubics, uniformly along the actual
construction as $t\to\infty$, that
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:asymptotic}
 \bl(\mathcal H)=\gamma 2^{12t}\bigl(1+O(2^{-t/2})\bigr),\qquad
 \log_2\log_2\mathcal H=12t+\log_2\gamma+o(1),
\end{equation}
where $\gamma=4/3$ in Branch A and $\gamma=25/14$ in Branch B.
The branch is fixed by the compiler's mask residue. These asymptotics
supplement the exact formulas rather than supply their floors.

For a fully explicit envelope, direct subtraction in the three cubics
shows for $R\ge100$ that
\[
 \tfrac54R^3<\bl(\mathcal H)<2R^3.
\]
For example the weakest lower polynomial, Branch A with
$\epsilon=-1$, differs from $\tfrac54R^3$ by
$R^3/12-4R^2+3R+2/3>0$ for $R\ge100$; all other lower
comparisons and upper comparisons are larger-margin coefficient
inequalities. From $R>(99/100)q^4$ and
$(5/4)(99/100)^3>1$, we conclude
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:tbound}
 2^{12t}<\bl(\mathcal H)<2^{12t+1},\qquad
 2^{\,2^{12t}}<\mathcal H<2^{\,2^{12t+1}}.
\end{equation}
For the strict lower height endpoint, use integrality:
$\bl(\mathcal H)>2^{12t}$ implies $N\ge2^{12t}$, then
$\mathcal H>2^N$. There is no omitted third exponential level.

With the least allowed radix, \eqref{fup:eh:eq:radixenvelope} gives the
all-input, compiler-dependent bounds
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:globalinput}
 2^{\,2^{12\max\{d,4M_*\}}}<\mathcal H
 <2^{\,2^{12\max\{d,20M_*\}+1}}.
\end{equation}
For every fixed compiler, eventually $I$ dominates $\bl(K)$ and $61$,
and $4I>d$. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:eventualinput}
 2^{\,2^{48I}}<\mathcal H<2^{\,2^{240I+1}},\qquad I=\ell x+b.
\end{equation}
The variable $x$ here is the numerical input value, not its binary
encoding length. Thus the double-exponential description refers to $t$
and, eventually for a fixed compiler, the input value $x$; it is not a
double-exponential bound in $\bl(x)$.
These are bounds, not a smooth leading asymptotic in $x$. For example
with $a=1$, $M_*=156$ allows $t=625$ while $M_*=157$ forces
$t=3125$. On each radix plateau the actual $R$ can still change through
packing data. Replacing $t$ everywhere by one constant multiple of $x$
would erase a real feature of the construction.

\section{Convergent log-height expansion and a controlled inverse}
\label{fup:eh:sec:expansion}
This section incorporates a separately completed and independently
audited extension \cite{fup:eh:expansion,fup:eh:expansionaudit}. The forward result
is an absolutely convergent identity in five dependent small arguments.
The inverse result combines a convergent algebraic cubic inverse with
the first exponentially small correction. It is not a complete
all-orders exponential inverse expansion.

\subsection{An exact identity and its convergent expansion}
Keep the displayed completion, and write
\begin{gather*}
 \lambda_A=A+\sqrt{A^2-1},\quad
 c=\frac{\lambda_A^p-\lambda_A^{-p}}{2\sqrt{A^2-1}},\quad
 S=\frac{(\lambda_A^p-\lambda_A^{-p})^2}{4},\\
 \lambda_S=S+\sqrt{S^2-1},\quad
 B_*=2p(R-1),\quad a_*=A^{-2},\quad b_*=S^{-2},\quad
 u_*=\lambda_A^{-2p},\quad v_*=\lambda_S^{-2R}.
\end{gather*}
The symbols $B_*,a_*,b_*$ are local analytic variables, not the
compiler radix or fixed compiler ports. Let
\[
 g(z)=\ln\frac{1+\sqrt{1-z}}2\qquad(0\le z<1),\qquad
 c_j=\frac{\binom{2j}{j}}{2j\,4^j}.
\]
All of $d_0,a_*,b_*,u_*,v_*$ belong to $(0,1)$.

\begin{theorem}[Exact forward log-height formula]\label{fup:eh:thm:forward}
The exact identity
\begin{align}
 (\ln2)(\log_2\mathcal H-N)
 &=B_*\bigl[\ln(1+d_0)+g(a_*)\bigr]
   +2(R-1)\ln(1-u_*)\nonumber\\
 &\quad+R g(b_*)-\tfrac12\ln(1-b_*)+\ln(1-v_*)\label{fup:eh:eq:exactlog}
\end{align}
has the absolutely convergent all-orders expansion
\begin{align}
 (\ln2)(\log_2\mathcal H-N)
 =\sum_{j\ge1}\biggl[&B_*\frac{(-1)^{j+1}d_0^j}{j}
 -B_*c_j a_*^j-\frac{2(R-1)u_*^j}{j}\nonumber\\
 &+\Bigl(\frac1{2j}-Rc_j\Bigr)b_*^j-\frac{v_*^j}{j}\biggr].
 \label{fup:eh:eq:series}
\end{align}
After truncation at $j=k\ge1$, the absolute error in natural-log
units is at most
\begin{align}
 \mathcal R_k={}&\frac{B_*d_0^{k+1}}{k+1}
 +\frac{B_*a_*^{k+1}}{2(k+1)(1-a_*)}
 +\frac{2(R-1)u_*^{k+1}}{(k+1)(1-u_*)}\nonumber\\
 &+\frac{(R+1)b_*^{k+1}}{2(k+1)(1-b_*)}
 +\frac{v_*^{k+1}}{(k+1)(1-v_*)}.\label{fup:eh:eq:tail}
\end{align}
Divide this bound by $\ln2$ for the error in $\log_2\mathcal H$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The identities
\[
 \lambda_A=2^L(1+d_0)e^{g(a_*)},\qquad
 2S=\lambda_A^{2p}(1-u_*)^2/2,
\]
and the auxiliary Pell formula give
\[
 \ln\mathcal H=(R-1)\ln(2S)+R g(b_*)
 -\tfrac12\ln(1-b_*)+\ln(1-v_*).
\]
Substitution proves \eqref{fup:eh:eq:exactlog}, including the coefficient
$R$ on $g(b_*)$ and the denominator correction.
Differentiating gives
\[
 g'(z)=-\frac{(1-z)^{-1/2}-1}{2z},
\]
so integration of the convergent binomial series with $g(0)=0$
yields $g(z)=-\sum_{j\ge1}c_jz^j$. The logarithm series then
prove \eqref{fup:eh:eq:series}; each component is absolutely convergent.
For \eqref{fup:eh:eq:tail}, use the alternating remainder for $\ln(1+d_0)$,
$0<c_j\le1/(2j)$, and geometric tail bounds for the other arguments.
For the $b_*$ coefficient the triangle inequality gives
$|1/(2j)-Rc_j|\le(R+1)/(2j)$, requiring no guessed sign.
\end{proof}

This formula separates main and auxiliary radical corrections from
their conjugate-power corrections. Grouping them by $j$ does not
assert a single global asymptotic ordering in the ordinary input, nor
remove the radix jumps. The arguments are dependent; that dependence
does not affect convergence of the finite sum of convergent series.

\begin{proposition}[Uniform leading correction]\label{fup:eh:prop:leading}
For the family, and for the real interpolation below,
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:leading}
 \left|\log_2\mathcal H-N-\frac{B_*d_0}{\ln2}\right|
 <\frac{B_*d_0^2}{\ln2}.
\end{equation}
In particular $\log_2\mathcal H-N>0$, and
\[
 \log_2\mathcal H-N=
 \frac{2p(R-1)}{\ln2}\,2^{-p}
 \bigl(1+2^{1-y}+O(d_0)\bigr).
\]
The constant-one absolute remainder is the explicit statement
\eqref{fup:eh:eq:leading}, before this rearrangement.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Under $p\ge16$, $y\ge3$, $R\ge8$, $R<2p$, one has
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:smallarguments}
 a_*,u_*,b_*,v_*\le d_0^2/16.
\end{equation}
Indeed $a_*<2^{-2p-2y}$ and $d_0\ge2^{-p}$.
Also $\lambda_A>2XY+1=2^L+1$, so
$u_*<2^{-2pL}$. For real as well as integral $p$, convexity gives
\[
 \lambda_A^{2p}>2^{2pL}+2p\,2^{(2p-1)L}>2^{2pL}+2,
\]
whence $S>2^{2pL-2}$, $b_*<2^{-4pL+4}$, and
$v_*<2^{-2R(2pL-2)}$ since $\lambda_S>S$.
Each of the latter three is at most $2^{-2p-4}$ by the stated
hypotheses, proving \eqref{fup:eh:eq:smallarguments}.

Use $|\ln(1+d_0)-d_0|\le d_0^2/2$,
$|g(z)|\le z/[2(1-z)]$, and
$|\ln(1-z)|\le z/(1-z)$. All four small arguments are below
$1/2$. In \eqref{fup:eh:eq:exactlog}, the five error contributions after
division by $B_*d_0^2$ are bounded respectively by
\[
 \frac12,\qquad\frac1{16},\qquad\frac1{8p},\qquad
 \frac1{16p},\qquad\frac18.
\]
Their sum is at most $179/256<1$ for $p\ge16$.
This proves \eqref{fup:eh:eq:leading}; positivity follows from $d_0<1$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Real analytic interpolation and the cubic branch}
For each branch separately, extend $p,y$ to affine real functions on
$R\ge100$:
\[
\begin{array}{c|cc}
&p(R)&y(R)\\\hline
\mathrm A+&2(R+1)/3&(R+1)/3-1\\
\mathrm A-&2(R-1)/3&(R-1)/3-1\\
\mathrm B&5(R+1)/7&15(R+1)/28-1
\end{array}
\]
Use the positive real formulas above to define $S(R)$ and
\[
 \mathcal H(R)=\frac{\sinh(R\theta(R))}{\sinh\theta(R)},\qquad
 \theta(R)=\operatorname{arcosh}S(R)>0.
\]
At the integer family parameters this agrees exactly with the Pell
witness. It is an analytic interpolation, not a claim that noninteger
parameters are chart witnesses.

$A,\lambda_A,\lambda_A^p$, and then $S$ strictly increase with $R$.
For fixed $\theta>0$, the hyperbolic quotient strictly increases
with $R>1$. For fixed $R>1$, its logarithmic derivative in $\theta$
is $R\coth(R\theta)-\coth\theta>0$, since
\[
 \frac{d}{dz}(z\coth z)
 =\frac{\sinh z\cosh z-z}{\sinh^2z}>0\qquad(z>0).
\]
Thus $\mathcal H(R)$ and $\log_2\mathcal H(R)$ are strictly
increasing on each branch, independently of integer bit-length results.

Let $N(R)$ be the cubic bit length minus one:
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:Ncubics}
\begin{array}{ll}
\mathrm A+:&(4R^3+4R^2-7R-1)/3,\\
\mathrm A-:&(4R^3-12R^2+9R-1)/3,\\
\mathrm B:&(25R^3+25R^2-39R-11)/14.
\end{array}
\end{equation}
Direct differentiation gives, uniformly for $R\ge100$,
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:derivativebudget}
 N'(R)>3R^2,\quad 0<N''(R)<12R,\quad
 B_*(R)<2R^2,\quad 0<B_*'(R)/B_*(R)\le3/R.
\end{equation}
For example $B_*$ is respectively
$\frac43(R^2-1)$, $\frac43(R-1)^2$, and $\frac{10}{7}(R^2-1)$.
The cubic is increasing and unbounded on this interval.

\begin{theorem}[One exponential inverse correction]\label{fup:eh:thm:inverse}
Fix an actual or interpolated $R\ge100$, write
$\ell_h=\log_2\mathcal H(R)$, and let $r_0\ge100$ be the
unique root $N(r_0)=\ell_h$. Put $p_0=p(r_0)$, $y_0=y(r_0)$,
and
\[
 \mathcal T(r)=\frac{2p(r)(r-1)}{(\ln2)N'(r)}\,2^{-p(r)}.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:inverse64}
 R=r_0-\mathcal T(r_0)+\varepsilon_h,\qquad
 |\varepsilon_h|\le64\bigl(2^{-p_0-y_0}+2^{-2p_0}\bigr).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write $\ell_h=N(R)+E(R)$, where \eqref{fup:eh:eq:leading} makes
$E(R)>0$. Thus $r_0>R$. For $\delta_r=r_0-R$ the mean-value
theorem gives $\delta_r=E(R)/N'(\xi)$, $R<\xi<r_0$.
Using \eqref{fup:eh:eq:derivativebudget}, $\ln2>2/3$, and
\eqref{fup:eh:eq:leading} gives $0<\delta_r<2d_0<1$.
Put $q_*=2^{-p(R)}$. Since $d_0=q_*(1+2/Y)<2q_*$,
\[
 \left|E(R)-\frac{B_*(R)q_*}{\ln2}\right|
 \le\frac{B_*(R)}{\ln2}\bigl(2q_*/Y+4q_*^2\bigr).
\]
Also $B_*/((\ln2)N')<1$. Division by $N'(\xi)$ therefore
contributes error at most $2q_*/Y+4q_*^2$.
Replacing its principal denominator $N'(\xi)$ by $N'(R)$
contributes at most
\[
 \frac{B_*q_*}{\ln2}
 \frac{N'(\xi)-N'(R)}{N'(\xi)N'(R)}
 <\frac{4q_*\delta_r}{R}<\frac{20q_*^2}{R}\le q_*^2/5.
\]
Here $N'(\xi)-N'(R)<12\xi(\xi-R)$ and
$\delta_r<4q_*$ suffice; the displayed $20$ is deliberately loose.
Furthermore
\[
 0<\mathcal T(r)<2^{-p(r)},\qquad
 |\mathcal T'/\mathcal T|
 \le B_*'/B_*+N''/N'+p'\ln2<7/r+1<2.
\]
Thus changing $\mathcal T(R)$ to $\mathcal T(r_0)$ costs less
than $2q_*\delta_r<8q_*^2$. Summing the errors gives less than
$2q_*/Y+13q_*^2$.
Finally $p',y'<1$ and $\delta_r<1$ imply
$q_*<2\,2^{-p_0}$ and $Y^{-1}<2\,2^{-y_0}$.
The total is bounded by
$8\,2^{-p_0-y_0}+52\,2^{-2p_0}$, proving the stated looser
constant $64$.
\end{proof}

The full rational prefactor in $\mathcal T(r)$ tends to
$1/(3\ln2)$ in either A branch and $4/(15\ln2)$ in B.
Replacing it by this limit incurs an extra
$O(2^{-p_0}/r_0)$ term, generally too large to preserve
\eqref{fup:eh:eq:inverse64}. Its evaluation point and full coefficient matter.

\subsection{Convergent algebraic inverse to all orders}
Let $\gamma=4/3$ in A and $\gamma=25/14$ in B, and put
$s_h=(\ell_h/\gamma)^{1/3}$. The large cubic root has
\begin{align*}
 \mathrm A+:\quad r_0&=s_h-\tfrac13+\tfrac{25}{36}s_h^{-1}
                         -\tfrac{11}{81}s_h^{-2}+O(s_h^{-3}),\\
 \mathrm A-:\quad r_0&=s_h+1+\tfrac14s_h^{-1}
                         +0\,s_h^{-2}+O(s_h^{-3}),\\
 \mathrm B:\quad r_0&=s_h-\tfrac13+\tfrac{142}{225}s_h^{-1}
                         -\tfrac{104}{2025}s_h^{-2}+O(s_h^{-3}).
\end{align*}
Direct substitution into \eqref{fup:eh:eq:Ncubics} proves the displayed
coefficients. For convergence at all algebraic orders write
$N(r)=\gamma r^3+b_2r^2+b_1r+b_0$, $r=s_h h$, $w_h=1/s_h$.
The equation becomes
\[
 h^3+(b_2/\gamma)w_hh^2+(b_1/\gamma)w_h^2h
 +(b_0/\gamma)w_h^3=1.
\]
At $(w_h,h)=(0,1)$ its derivative in $h$ is three. The analytic
implicit-function theorem gives a unique convergent power series
$h(w_h)$ near zero; its recursively determined coefficients supply
all algebraic orders of the large real cubic root. No explicit
convergence radius is asserted. Theorem~\ref{fup:eh:thm:inverse} adds the
first controlled exponential correction beyond this algebraic branch.

\section{A convergent all-orders analytic inverse algorithm}
\label{fup:eh:sec:allinverse}
A further separately audited extension \cite{fup:eh:allinverse,fup:eh:allaudit}
supplies an all-orders analytic inverse with an explicit geometric
tail. It is a Lagrange derivative algorithm in an auxiliary parameter;
it does not claim that every inverse exponential sector has been
rearranged into a canonical ordered transseries.

\subsection{Holomorphic formulas without logarithmic winding}
Fix one branch and a real center $r_0\ge100$. On the closed complex
disk $|r-r_0|\le1/4$, write
\[
 p(r)=\alpha_*(r+\epsilon),\quad L(r)=\lambda_*(r+\epsilon),\quad
 y(r)=L(r)-p(r)-1,
\]
where $(\alpha_*,\lambda_*,\epsilon)$ is
$(2/3,1,1)$, $(2/3,1,-1)$, or $(5/7,5/4,1)$.
Put $p_0=p(r_0)$, $y_0=y(r_0)$, $q_0=2^{-p_0}$.
Here $2^z$ means $\exp(z\ln2)$. Define, in this order,
\begin{align*}
 d_c&=2^{-p}+2\,2^{-p-y},&
 a_c&=2^{-2p-2y}(1+d_c)^{-2},\\
 \ell_A&=L\ln2+\ln(1+d_c)+g(a_c),&
 u_c&=\exp(-2p\ell_A),\\
 b_c&=16u_c^2/(1-u_c)^4,&
 \ell_S&=2p\ell_A-\ln2+2\ln(1-u_c)+g(b_c),\\
 v_c&=\exp(-2r\ell_S).&&
\end{align*}
The logarithms $\ln(1+z)$ and $\ln(1-z)$ are their Taylor branches
at zero, and $g$ is the convergent series from
Theorem~\ref{fup:eh:thm:forward}. These formulas specify holomorphic
logarithms $\ell_A,\ell_S$; they never take a principal
$\log S$ or principal $\operatorname{arcosh}S$ on the complex disk,
where the values of $S$ may wind around zero.
Let $N(r)$ be the branch cubic \eqref{fup:eh:eq:Ncubics}, and define
\begin{align}
 E_c(r)=\frac1{\ln2}\bigl\{&2p(r)(r-1)[\ln(1+d_c)+g(a_c)]
 +2(r-1)\ln(1-u_c)\nonumber\\
 &+r g(b_c)-\tfrac12\ln(1-b_c)+\ln(1-v_c)\bigr\}.
 \label{fup:eh:eq:complexE}
\end{align}
On the real axis this is exactly $\log_2\mathcal H(r)-N(r)$.
We prove all required branch and nonvanishing assertions, rather than
assuming that these formulas are well defined.

\begin{lemma}[Uniform complex-disk bounds]\label{fup:eh:lem:disk}
On a neighborhood of $|r-r_0|\le1/4$, $E_c$ and
\[
 D_c(r;r_0)=\frac{N(r)-N(r_0)}{r-r_0},\qquad
 D_c(r_0;r_0)=N'(r_0),\qquad \mathfrak f(r)=E_c(r)/D_c(r;r_0)
\]
are holomorphic, and on the closed disk
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:diskbounds}
 |E_c(r)|<18r_0^2q_0,\qquad |D_c(r;r_0)|>2r_0^2,\qquad
 |\mathfrak f(r)|<\varepsilon_0:=16q_0<1/8.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Uniformly, $p_0\ge66$, $y_0\ge32$, and $|p(r)|<r_0$.
The slopes of $p,y$ are positive and less than one, so
\[
 |2^{-p}|\le2^{1/4}q_0<(5/4)q_0,\quad
 |2^{-y}|<(5/4)2^{-y_0},\quad |d_c|<2q_0\le2^{-65}.
\]
Also $|2^{-2p-2y}|<4q_0^2\,2^{-2y_0}$ and
$|1+d_c|^{-2}<2$, whence $|a_c|<q_0^2$.
The convergent series give, with $\Phi=\ln(1+d_c)+g(a_c)$,
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:Phi}
 |\ln(1+d_c)|<4q_0,\qquad |g(a_c)|<q_0^2,\qquad
 |\Phi|<5q_0.
\end{equation}

Write $h_c=r-r_0$ and $z_0=r_0+\epsilon$. The inequality
$\operatorname{Re}((z_0+h_c)^2)\ge z_0^2-2z_0|h_c|-|h_c|^2$
and $2/3\le\alpha_*\lambda_*\le25/28$ imply
\[
 \operatorname{Re}(pL)\ge\frac23
 \left(r_0^2-\frac52r_0+\frac7{16}\right)\ge\frac35r_0^2.
\]
The last comparison follows by direct subtraction for $r_0\ge100$.
As $5r_0q_0<1$, \eqref{fup:eh:eq:Phi} gives
\[
 \operatorname{Re}(p\ell_A)\ge\tfrac35r_0^2\ln2-1
 >\tfrac12r_0^2\ln2.
\]
Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:ubdisk}
 |u_c|<2^{-r_0^2}\le q_0^4,\qquad
 |b_c|\le\frac{16q_0^8}{(1-q_0^4)^4}<32q_0^8<q_0^4.
\end{equation}
Here $p_0<r_0$ and $r_0^2\ge4r_0$ justify the first comparison.
This validates the Taylor branches involving $u_c,b_c$. In particular
\[
 \ell_S=2pL\ln2-\ln2+Q_c,\qquad
 Q_c=2p\Phi+2\ln(1-u_c)+g(b_c),\qquad |Q_c|<11r_0q_0.
\]

The cubic $r(r+\epsilon)^2$ differs from its center value by at most
\[
 \tfrac14(3r_0^2+4r_0+1)+\tfrac1{16}(3r_0+2)+\tfrac1{64}<r_0^2.
\]
Since $\alpha_*\lambda_*<1$, it follows that
\[
 \operatorname{Re}(rpL)\ge\tfrac23r_0(r_0-1)^2-r_0^2
 \ge\tfrac35r_0^3.
\]
Using $22r_0^2q_0<1$ and $|r|\le r_0+1/4$ gives
\[
 \operatorname{Re}(r\ell_S)
 \ge\tfrac65r_0^3\ln2-(r_0+1/4)\ln2-1>r_0^3\ln2,
 \qquad |v_c|<2^{-2r_0^3}\le q_0^4.
\]
The elementary inequalities $5r_0q_0<1$ and $22r_0^2q_0<1$
hold at $r_0=100$ using $q_0\le2^{-66}$; their left sides
decrease thereafter because $p_0'\ge2/3$.

Now $|2p(r-1)|<2r_0^2$ and $|r-1|<r_0$.
The first numerator term in \eqref{fup:eh:eq:complexE} is less than
$10r_0^2q_0$. By \eqref{fup:eh:eq:ubdisk}, the remaining terms have
total modulus below
\[
 4r_0q_0^4+2r_0q_0^4+q_0^4+2q_0^4
 =(6r_0+3)q_0^4<r_0^2q_0.
\]
Since $1/\ln2<3/2$, this gives
$|E_c|<(33/2)r_0^2q_0<18r_0^2q_0$.

If $\gamma<2$ is the cubic's leading coefficient, then exactly
\[
 D_c(r_0+h_c;r_0)=N'(r_0)+\tfrac12N''(r_0)h_c+\gamma h_c^2.
\]
The real derivative bounds imply
$|D_c|>3r_0^2-(3/2)r_0-1/8>2r_0^2$.
Thus $D_c$ never vanishes and $|\mathfrak f|<9q_0<16q_0$.
All series arguments have modulus strictly below one, with strict
uniform bounds on the compact disk. Those bounds persist on a
slightly larger disk, proving holomorphy on a neighborhood of its closure.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Lagrange inverse with an explicit tail]\label{fup:eh:thm:lagrange}
Put $\rho_0=1/(8\varepsilon_0)>1$. The unique small solution of
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:himplicit}
 h(z)=-z\,\mathfrak f(r_0+h(z)),\qquad h(0)=0,
\end{equation}
is holomorphic on a neighborhood of $|z|\le\rho_0$ and has
$|h(z)|<1/4$ there. Its convergent Lagrange series is
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:lagrange}
 h(z)=\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{(-z)^n}{n!}
 \left.\frac{d^{n-1}}{dr^{n-1}}\bigl[\mathfrak f(r)^n\bigr]
 \right|_{r=r_0}.
\end{equation}
At $z=1$, $r=r_0+h(1)$ is the exact inverse of
$\log_2\mathcal H(r)=N(r_0)$ in the disk. For every $K\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:lagrangetail}
 \left|h(1)-\sum_{n=1}^K\frac{(-1)^n}{n!}
 \left.\frac{d^{n-1}}{dr^{n-1}}[\mathfrak f(r)^n]\right|_{r=r_0}\right|
 \le\frac14\frac{\rho_0^{-K-1}}{1-\rho_0^{-1}}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
On $|h|=1/4$ and $|z|\le\rho_0$,
$|z\mathfrak f(r_0+h)|<\rho_0\varepsilon_0=1/8<|h|$.
Rouch\'e's theorem gives exactly one zero, counting multiplicity,
of $h+z\mathfrak f(r_0+h)$ in the disk. It is therefore simple.
The analytic implicit-function theorem, together with uniqueness,
patches the local solution into one holomorphic function throughout
the parameter disk and its neighborhood. Lagrange inversion at zero
gives \eqref{fup:eh:eq:lagrange}. At $z=1$, multiplying
\eqref{fup:eh:eq:himplicit} by $D_c(r_0+h;r_0)$ gives
$N(r_0+h)+E_c(r_0+h)=N(r_0)$, the desired inverse equation.
Finally Cauchy's coefficient estimate on $|z|=\rho_0$ is
$|[z^n]h(z)|\le(1/4)\rho_0^{-n}$. Summing its geometric tail
proves \eqref{fup:eh:eq:lagrangetail} and absolute convergence at $z=1$.
\end{proof}

For an actual $R\ge100$, take $N(r_0)=\log_2\mathcal H(R)$
as in Theorem~\ref{fup:eh:thm:inverse}. Its localization gives
$0<r_0-R<2d_0<1/4$, so this real $R$ is the unique root above;
conjugation symmetry also makes $h(1)$ real. At an arbitrary center
$r_0=100$, the inverse of the target $N(100)$ may instead lie
slightly below $100$. The disk theorem then concerns the local
analytic continuation, not an inverse constrained to the half-line
$R\ge100$. This endpoint caveat does not affect the application
starting from an actual $R\ge100$, where $r_0>R\ge100$.

The first Lagrange term is $-E_c(r_0)/N'(r_0)$ and retains all
forward scales. Its leading $2^{-p_0}$ part agrees with
Theorem~\ref{fup:eh:thm:inverse}, while \eqref{fup:eh:eq:lagrangetail} makes
the $n\ge2$ terms uniformly $O(q_0^2)$.
Every finite derivative in \eqref{fup:eh:eq:lagrange} is computable by
power-series arithmetic on the explicitly specified holomorphic
functions. This is a genuinely convergent all-orders algorithm with
a computable error bound. Its ordering is by the artificial parameter
$z$, and it does not smooth the input's radix jumps.

\section{All positive auxiliary completions and unique minimality}
\label{fup:eh:sec:minimum}
We now hold the entire outer tuple fixed and allow every positive
auxiliary choice in \eqref{fup:eh:eq:82aux}. First,
$A\equiv2\pmod4$ and $p\equiv0\pmod4$. Modulo eight, the
$\psi_A$ recurrence repeats $0,1,4,7$, so $8\mid c$. Moreover
\[
 c\ge(2A-1)^{p-1}\ge3^{p-1}>4p>2R
\]
for $p\ge12$. In particular $0<R<c/2$.

\begin{lemma}[Integer-coordinate Pell classification]\label{fup:eh:lem:pellclass}
For integral $S\ge2$, every solution of
$x^2-(S^2-1)y_*^2=1$ with $x,y_*>0$ is
$(x,y_*)=(\chi_S(m),\psi_S(m))$ for a unique $m\ge1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The discriminant is strictly between $(S-1)^2$ and $S^2$.
For $y_*=1$ necessarily $x=S$. For $y_*>1$,
$\sqrt{S^2-1}\,y_*<x<Sy_*$ and $x>(S-1)y_*$.
Multiplication by $S-\sqrt{S^2-1}$ gives integral coordinates
\[
 x'=Sx-(S^2-1)y_*>0,\qquad 0<y_*'=Sy_*-x<y_*.
\]
They have the same norm. Descent ends at $(S,1)$ and then $(1,0)$;
reversal proves the classification, and strict growth gives uniqueness.
Only the integer-coordinate Pell problem is used, not a claim about
all algebraic integers of the quadratic field.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Odd quotient polynomial]\label{fup:eh:lem:oddpoly}
There are integer polynomials $Q_h$ such that
\[
 \chi_S(2h+1)=S Q_h(S^2),\quad
 Q_0(Z)=1,\quad Q_1(Z)=4Z-3,\quad
 Q_{h+2}=(4Z-2)Q_{h+1}-Q_h.
\]
They satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:Qidentities}
 Q_h(0)=(-1)^h(2h+1),\qquad
 Q_h(1-A^2)=(-1)^h\psi_A(2h+1).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The step-two recurrence for $\chi$ proves the first assertion and
the two initial values. The constant term follows by induction.
For the second identity, the sequence $(-1)^h\psi_A(2h+1)$ has
initial values $1,1-4A^2$ and recurrence coefficient $2-4A^2$,
exactly the specialized polynomial recurrence.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Complete auxiliary classification]\label{fup:eh:prop:allaux}
For the fixed Report45 outer tuple, all positive completions are
parametrized by an arbitrary $i\in\pos$ and a positive rank
$m\equiv R$ or $-R\pmod c$. They are exactly
\begin{align*}
 S&=i\D c^2,\quad \Fa=\D i^2c^4+1,\quad
 \yaux=\ps S m,\quad V=\ch S m/S,\\
 \Ua&=(V+R\Fa)/c+1.
\end{align*}
Their least rank is $R$ and their next rank is $c-R$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Because $P_5=1$, the zero condition is $\Na Q_s=1$.
Both factors are integers. Since $4\mid S$,
$\Na\equiv\yaux^2\pmod4$, so $\Na=-1$ is impossible.
Necessarily $\Na=Q_s=1$, forcing the stated $\Fa$.
Apply Lemma~\ref{fup:eh:lem:pellclass} to $x=|SV|$ and $\yaux>0$;
this yields $SV=\pm\chi_S(m)$ and $\yaux=\psi_S(m)$.

The recurrence modulo $S$ gives
$\chi_S(2j)\equiv(-1)^j$ and $\chi_S(2j+1)\equiv0$.
Therefore $m$ must be odd. Writing $m=2h+1$, let
$v_m=\chi_S(m)/S>0$. Since $c\mid S$, Lemma~\ref{fup:eh:lem:oddpoly}
implies $v_m\equiv(-1)^h m\pmod c$, and hence
$v_m\equiv1\pmod4$. On the other hand the literal $V$ relation
requires $V\equiv-R\equiv1\pmod4$, since
$\Fa\equiv1\pmod c$ and $R\equiv3\pmod4$.
Thus $V=-v_m$ is impossible; all signs have been treated.

For positive $V$, the congruence holds precisely when
$m\equiv R\pmod c$ with $m\equiv3\pmod4$, or
$m\equiv-R\pmod c$ with $m\equiv1\pmod4$.
As $4\mid c$, these are simply the stated two residue classes.
They make $\Ua$ integral and positive, and the resulting norm and
strong factor are one, proving sufficiency as well as necessity.
Finally $0<R<c/2$ identifies the first two positive ranks as
$R,c-R$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{fup:eh:thm:min}]
At fixed $S$, both Pell coordinates strictly increase with rank.
For $m\ge2$,
\[
 \psi_S(m)=\sum_{\substack{1\le k\le m\\k\text{ odd}}}
 \binom mk S^{m-k}(S^2-1)^{(k-1)/2}
\]
is strictly increasing in real $S\ge2$: all terms are nondecreasing
and the $k=1$ term is strictly increasing. For odd $m\ge3$,
\[
 \chi_S(m)/S=\sum_{\substack{0\le k\le m-1\\k\text{ even}}}
 \binom mk S^{m-k-1}(S^2-1)^{k/2}
\]
has the same property. Every admissible rank is at least $R\ge3$,
and $S=i\D c^2$ increases strictly with $i$.
Thus $\yaux$ and $V$ are strictly larger unless $i=1,m=R$.
The strong witness strictly increases with $i$, and
$\Ua=(V+R\Fa)/c+1$ has the same unique minimum.
$i$ itself is minimal at one and $\Fa$ at every admissible rank
with $i=1$, establishing the simultaneous-coordinate assertion.

Let $\mathcal H_*=\psi_{\D c^2}(R)$. Every completion has
$\yaux\ge\mathcal H_*$, strictly unless it is the displayed one.
Theorem~\ref{fup:eh:thm:height} shows that this displayed $\yaux$ strictly
exceeds all fixed outer coordinates and the other three auxiliaries.
Hence it attains the minimum of the maximum of all 18 coordinates,
and every other completion has strictly larger maximum.
Proposition~\ref{fup:eh:prop:allaux} fixes all remaining coordinates uniquely.
\end{proof}

\section{Small-norm descent for the free83 interface}
\label{fup:eh:sec:smallnorm}
To make the nonextension theorem self-contained, we prove the exact
arithmetic lemma imported by its original research packet from
Report43 \cite{fup:eh:smallnorm,fup:eh:freeaudit}. The variable $y_*$ below is the
positive auxiliary coordinate, not the construction exponent $y$.

\begin{writenote}
\emph{Second proof (source~20).} The next lemma is Lemma~\ref{fup:fc:lem:smallnorm} of Report~43, with that lemma's $H_0$, $N$ and $y$ written $H$, $N_*$ and $y_*$; Report~46 restates and proves it to keep Theorem~\ref{fup:eh:thm:free} self-contained, and credits Report~43~\cite{fup:eh:smallnorm}. The two descents are the same transformation~\eqref{fup:fc:eq:descent}. The programme's review read Report~43's version directly.
\end{writenote}
\begin{lemma}[Small norms and the negative equality orbit]
\label{fup:eh:lem:smallnorm}
Let $H\ge2$, $v\ge0$, $y_*>0$ be integers and
$N_*=Hv^2-(H-1)y_*^2$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $N_*<0$, then $N_*\le-(H-1)$.
\item If $0<N_*<H$, then $N_*$ is a positive perfect square.
\item If $N_*=-(H-1)$, every nonnegative-$v$ solution is
\[
 v=2(H-1)\psi_{2H-1}(r),\qquad y_*=\chi_{2H-1}(r),\qquad r\ge0.
\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $N_*<0$, then $y_*>v$. Put $t_*=y_*-v\ge1$; then
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:smallidentity}
 -N_*=(H-1)t_*^2+v\bigl(2(H-1)t_*-v\bigr).
\end{equation}
If $v\le2(H-1)t_*$, this is at least $H-1$. Otherwise negativity
implies
\[
 v<\bigl(H-1+\sqrt{H(H-1)}\bigr)t_*<(2H-1)t_*.
\]
The integral transformation
\begin{align*}
 v'&=(2H-1)v-2(H-1)y_*=v-2(H-1)t_*,\\
 y_*'&=(2H-1)y_*-2Hv=(2H-1)t_*-v
\end{align*}
has $0<v'<v$, $y_*'>0$, and preserves $N_*$ by expansion.
Induction on $v$ proves the first assertion.

If $0<N_*<H$, then $v\ge y_*+1$ would give
$N_*=H(v^2-y_*^2)+y_*^2\ge H$, impossible. Thus $y_*\ge v$.
When $y_*=v$ the norm is $v^2$. Otherwise $t_*\ge1$ and
positivity in \eqref{fup:eh:eq:smallidentity} requires
$v>2(H-1)t_*$. Also $v\ge(2H-1)t_*$ would give
$N_*\ge Ht_*^2\ge H$, since the quadratic in $v$ is increasing
beyond its positive root. The same descent has positive coordinates
and smaller $v$, so eventually $y_*=v$; the norm is a square.

For $N_*=-(H-1)$, descend while $v>2(H-1)t_*$.
At a terminal point \eqref{fup:eh:eq:smallidentity} forces $t_*=1$ and
$v=0$ or $2(H-1)$. The latter maps to $(v',y_*')=(0,1)$.
The inverse transformation has matrix
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}2H-1&2(H-1)\\2H&2H-1\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Its iterates on $(0,1)$ are exactly the displayed Pell formula,
as verified by the initial value and recurrence. Conversely every such
iterate has the required norm. This includes $v=0$ at $r=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Exhaustion of norm signs]\label{fup:eh:prop:signs}
Under \eqref{fup:eh:eq:freenorms}--\eqref{fup:eh:eq:freeproduct} with $A\ge4$,
$f,S,y_*>0$, one has $S\ge2$. If $\Na>0$, then
$\Na=z^2$ for some positive integer $z$ with $z^2\mid\D$.
If $\Na<0$, then necessarily
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:negativebranch}
 f=1,\quad S=A,\quad \Ns=-1,\quad \Na=-\D,\quad
 V=\pm2\D\psi_{2A^2-1}(r)\quad(r\ge0).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Both norm factors are nonzero divisors of $\D$ and have the same
sign. If $S=1$, then $\Ns=\D f^2-1>1$ is coprime to $\D$,
contradicting divisibility. Set $H=S^2\ge4$.

Suppose $\Na<0$. The small-norm lemma gives
$|\Ns|(H-1)\le\D$, in particular $H-1\le\D$.
If $f\ge2$, the strong equation and $|\Ns|\le\D$ instead give
$H=\D f^2-\Ns\ge3\D$, a contradiction.
Thus $f=1$. Write $b_*=-\Ns\ge1$; then $H=\D+b_*$ and
$b_*(\D+b_*-1)\le\D$, forcing $b_*=1$.
Consequently $S=A$, $\Ns=-1$, $\Na=-\D$.
The equality orbit of Lemma~\ref{fup:eh:lem:smallnorm}, with $v=|V|$,
gives the last formula.

Suppose $\Na>0$; then $\Ns>0$. We show $\Na<H$.
If $f\ge2$, $H\ge3\D>\D/\Ns=\Na$.
If $f=1$, then $\D=H+\Ns$. The value $\Ns=1$ would imply
$A^2-S^2=2$, impossible for positive integers, so $\Ns\ge2$.
Now $\Ns H>H+\Ns=\D$, because $H\ge4$, giving
$H>\D/\Ns=\Na$ again. Lemma~\ref{fup:eh:lem:smallnorm} makes
$\Na$ a positive square, and its divisibility gives $z^2\mid\D$.
\end{proof}

\section{The nonsquarefree Pell lattice and residue separation}
\label{fup:eh:sec:lattice}
A tempting but incorrect shortcut is to assume every strong-$\D$
solution has $f=\chi_A(m)$ and $S=\D\psi_A(m)$. When $\D$
is nonsquarefree this can miss solutions. For example,
$A=26$, $\D=675$ has $f=2,S=45$ satisfying
$675\cdot2^2-45^2=675$; the least positive $\chi_{26}(m)$
is $26$, not $2$. The correct lattice is the following.

\begin{lemma}[Underlying integer Pell unit]\label{fup:eh:lem:lattice}
Let $A\ge4$ be even and $\D=d b_0^2$ with $d$ squarefree.
There exist even $a\ge2$, odd $b\ge1$, and odd $e\ge1$ with
\[
 a^2-db^2=1,\quad
 A+b_0\sqrt d=(a+b\sqrt d)^e.
\]
Put $C_*=\psi_a(e)$ and $\delta_*=a^2-1=db^2$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:latticeidentities}
 b_0=bC_*,\quad \D=\delta_*C_*^2,\quad
 C_*\psi_A(t)=\psi_a(et)\quad(t\ge0).
\end{equation}
Every positive strong-$\D$ solution is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:stronglattice}
 \D f^2-S^2=\D
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 f=\chi_a(m),\quad S=\delta_*C_*\psi_a(m),\quad m\ge1.
\end{equation}
Moreover $\gcd(\chi_a(m),b_0)=1$ for every $m\ge0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Since $\D\equiv3\pmod4$, both $b_0$ and $d$ are odd, and
$d\equiv3\pmod4$. Also $d>1$ because $\D$ is not a square.
Among positive integer-coordinate norm-one units for $d$, choose
the least one $u_0=a+b\sqrt d>1$; existence is supplied by
$A+b_0\sqrt d$. Every positive unit is a power of $u_0$:
choose $k$ with $u_0^k\le u<u_0^{k+1}$; the quotient is an
integer-coordinate norm-one unit in $[1,u_0)$, hence is one by
minimality. Its coordinates have the claimed signs because for a
positive real norm-one unit $w\ge1$, the conjugate is $w^{-1}$,
so its two coordinates are nonnegative. This proves generation
without requiring a classification of the full ring of integers.

If $a$ were odd, the norm equation modulo four would force $b$ even,
and all powers would have odd first coordinate, contrary to even $A$.
Thus $a$ is even, $b$ is odd, and modulo two the coefficients of
$u_0^k$ alternate between $(1,0)$ and $(0,1)$. Therefore the exponent
$e$ representing $A+b_0\sqrt d$ is odd. Comparing coordinates and
then powers proves \eqref{fup:eh:eq:latticeidentities}.

If $\D f^2-S^2=\D$, then $b_0^2\mid S^2$, so $b_0\mid S$.
Write $S=b_0s$; then $s^2=d(f^2-1)$, and squarefreeness gives
$s=dv$. Thus $f^2-dv^2=1$, and generation gives
$f=\chi_a(m)$, $v=b\psi_a(m)$ for $m\ge1$ because $S>0$.
This proves \eqref{fup:eh:eq:stronglattice}; its converse follows from the norm.

The norm also implies $\gcd(\chi_a(m),b)=1$. Suppose an odd prime
$\ell_0$ divides both $\chi_a(m)$ and $C_*$.
In the commutative ring
$\mathbb F_{\ell_0}[s]/(s^2-\delta_*)$, the element $u=a+s$
is a unit of norm one. The two divisibilities imply
$u^{2m}=-1$ and $u^{2e}=1$. Raising these identities to $e$ and
$m$, respectively, gives $-1=1$ because $e$ is odd, impossible.
This ring argument remains valid if its quadratic is reducible or has
a repeated root. The prime two does not divide $b_0=bC_*$.
Thus $\gcd(\chi_a(m),b_0)=1$ as claimed.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Opposite-parity Pell residues]\label{fup:eh:lem:parity}
For $a\ge2$, $m\ge1$, and $F_* =\chi_a(m)$, the sets
\[
 \{\pm\psi_a(t)\bmod F_*:t\ge0\text{ even}\},\qquad
 \{\pm\psi_a(t)\bmod F_*:t\ge0\text{ odd}\}
\]
are disjoint.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For the formal unit $u=a+\sqrt{a^2-1}$, its coefficientwise
identity modulo $F_*$ is $u^{2m}=-1$; this follows from the
norm and $\chi_a(m)\equiv0$. Reduction modulo $2m$ therefore
changes only a sign. Reflection of a reduced rank $t$ to $2m-t$
preserves the $\psi$ residue, because
$u^{2m-t}=-u^{-t}$ has second coefficient $\psi_a(t)$.
Both operations preserve parity. Hence every residue has a signed
representative $\pm\psi_a(j)$ with $0\le j\le m$ and
$j\equiv t\pmod2$.

For $m=1$ the representatives are zero and one modulo $a\ge2$.
For $m\ge2$ write $b_1=\psi_a(m)$, $b_2=\psi_a(m-1)$.
Strict increase and recurrence give $b_1>(2a-1)b_2$, while
$F_*=ab_1-b_2$. Consequently $b_1+b_2<F_*$:
indeed $F_*-(b_1+b_2)=(a-1)b_1-2b_2>0$.
Distinct-parity representatives have distinct indices, so a nonzero
difference has absolute value less than $F_*$ and a positive sum is
at most $b_1+b_2<F_*$. Neither can be zero modulo $F_*$.
Zero and nonzero representatives are included in this argument.
\end{proof}

\section{Proof of the free83 nonextension theorem}
\label{fup:eh:sec:freeproof}
Assume for contradiction that \eqref{fup:eh:eq:freeV}--\eqref{fup:eh:eq:freeproduct}
hold under Theorem~\ref{fup:eh:thm:free}'s hypotheses.

\subsection{Positive auxiliary norms reduce to one}
By Proposition~\ref{fup:eh:prop:signs}, $\Na=z^2>0$ with
$z^2\mid\D=d b_0^2$. As $d$ is squarefree, $z\mid b_0$.
Multiplying the strong equation by $z^2$ yields
\[
 \D(zf)^2-(zS)^2=\D.
\]
Lemma~\ref{fup:eh:lem:lattice} therefore gives $zf=\chi_a(m)$ for
some $m\ge1$. Its coprimality conclusion forces $z=1$.
Thus every positive branch has
\begin{equation}\label{fup:eh:eq:unitbranch}
 \Na=1,\quad \Ns=\D,\quad
 f=\chi_a(m),\quad S=\delta_*C_*\psi_a(m),\quad m\ge1.
\end{equation}
This is a proof of normalization for this interface, rather than an
assumed generic unit theorem for the full free83 polynomial.

\subsection{The unit branch contradicts rank parity}
By Lemma~\ref{fup:eh:lem:pellclass}, $\Na=1$ implies
\[
 SV=\varepsilon\chi_S(t_*),\quad y_*=\psi_S(t_*),\quad
 t_*\ge1,\quad \varepsilon\in\{+1,-1\}.
\]
Divisibility by $S$ forces $t_*$ odd. Write $t_*=2h+1$.
The strong identity makes $S^2\equiv-\D=1-A^2\pmod f$,
so Lemma~\ref{fup:eh:lem:oddpoly} gives
\[
 V\equiv\varepsilon Q_h(1-A^2)
 =\varepsilon(-1)^h\psi_A(t_*)\pmod f.
\]
The literal expression \eqref{fup:eh:eq:freeV} instead gives
$V\equiv-c=-\psi_A(p)\pmod f$. Multiplying the resulting signed
congruence by $C_*$, and using \eqref{fup:eh:eq:latticeidentities}, yields
\[
 \psi_a(et_*)\equiv\pm\psi_a(ep)\pmod{\chi_a(m)}.
\]
The first rank is odd because $e,t_*$ are odd; the second is even
because $p$ is even. This contradicts Lemma~\ref{fup:eh:lem:parity}.
We multiplied by $C_*$; no unproved division by $C_*$ modulo $f$
is involved. The value of $R$ disappears in this step, so this
positive-branch obstruction does not require $R$ to be odd.

\subsection{The negative branch contradicts the literal parity}
Proposition~\ref{fup:eh:prop:signs} forces the entire negative branch
\eqref{fup:eh:eq:negativebranch}, and in particular makes $V$ even.
But $f=1$ in \eqref{fup:eh:eq:freeV} gives
\[
 V=c(T-1)-R.
\]
The recurrence modulo two gives $\psi_A(p)\equiv p\pmod2$,
so $c$ is even, whereas $R$ is odd. The displayed expression for
$V$ is therefore odd, a contradiction. The equality orbit's
$V=0$ case is also excluded. Positive and negative branches exhaust
the nonzero product $\Na\Ns=\D>0$, proving
Theorem~\ref{fup:eh:thm:free}.

\subsection{Application to the authenticated Report45 tuples}
Those tuples have $A=Y(X+1)+2\equiv2\pmod4$,
$p\equiv0\pmod4$, $c=\psi_A(p)$, $R\equiv3\pmod4$, and
$P_5=1$, exactly as recorded in Section~\ref{fup:eh:sec:setup}.
Hence all satisfy the theorem's hypotheses. The independent free83
audit initially certified the arithmetic theorem conditionally on these
outer invariants because the historical construction directory was
unavailable. The recovered authenticated Report45 construction supplied
here verifies the invariants and closes that source-level application.
It does not extend the conclusion to other outer tuples.

\section{Verification, reproducibility, and limitations}
\label{fup:eh:sec:verification}
\subsection{Proofs and finite checks have different roles}
The unbounded conclusions are proved by Sections~\ref{fup:eh:sec:error}--
\ref{fup:eh:sec:freeproof}. Numerical tests corroborate them and expose
implementation or transcription mistakes; they do not prove the
quantifiers over genuine compilers, inputs, or auxiliary ranks.
No astronomical full tuple is materialized. Inner Pell component
fixtures and diagnostic outer CRT fixtures remain disjoint, and
diagnostic masks are never represented as genuine compiled programs.

The preserved author height packet \cite{fup:eh:height} reports 3,458 active
checks, six inner fixtures, 74 odd input-loader ranks, 27 separate
outer fixtures, 241 rational-error cases, and 401 cubic-envelope ranks.
Its largest materialized integer is 307,945 bits under a 500,000-bit
cap. Seven tamper scenarios, each under normal and optimized Python,
reject altered source pins, wrong exact floors, or a reduced hard cap.
The separate auxiliary checker treats sign/index classes, Pell descent,
modulo-four norm exclusion, and positive-$i$ monotonicity.

The independent height audit \cite{fup:eh:heightaudit} gives a mathematical
PASS and independently checks nine inner fixtures, 45 input-loader
ranks, 37 transport/pure-power cases, 29 outer fixtures, 11,640
minimal-radix cases, 5,703 cubic inequalities, 985 rational budgets,
69 exhaustively located Pell solutions, and 45 auxiliary-sector cases.
Its largest checked Pell coordinate is 389,069 bits under a
450,000-bit cap. It also proves the fixed-outer minimum-height
corollary used in Theorem~\ref{fup:eh:thm:min}.

The free83 research packet \cite{fup:eh:freeproof} supplies separate bounded
parity, odd-index coprimality, nonsquarefree-lattice, and strong-divisor
tests. Its independent proof audit \cite{fup:eh:freeaudit} is read-only
mathematical review; no nonexistent numerical audit script is implied.
The additional report-level checker corroborates the newly restated
small-norm descent and odd-polynomial identities. All active checks
use explicit failures, so Python optimization does not disable them.

The separately preserved analytic author checker makes 355 active
checks at 450 decimal digits, over nine real parameter cases. The
independent analytic checker makes 8,719 active checks, including 18
real cases through $R=512$ with fractional ranks, up to 983 decimal
digits, and 12 exact rational inverse-series coefficients. Its
checks of \eqref{fup:eh:eq:inverse64} have largest sampled error-to-bound
ratio below $0.0151$. These high-precision calculations are
corroboration, not interval-certified proofs. The uniform bounds are
proved analytically in Section~\ref{fup:eh:sec:expansion} and separately
audited \cite{fup:eh:expansionaudit}.

The all-orders inverse author checker supplies 82,094 active checks,
including 3,601 exact rational grid cases and 2,880 complex samples
over the three branches. It controls the tiny conjugates through
logarithmic bounds rather than treating floating underflow as exact
arithmetic. Its complex sampling is not interval certification;
Section~\ref{fup:eh:sec:allinverse} proves the uniform disk bounds and
convergent inverse directly.
Its independent checker contributes 26,373 active checks, including
2,409 exact rational branch/center cases, 864 complex samples, nine
real inverse cases through five Lagrange coefficients, and 36 roots
on the large artificial-parameter circle. Its largest sampled
geometric-tail error-to-bound ratio is below $0.01503$.

\subsection{Portable inspected replay}
The ZIP preserves the scientific packets byte for byte and inventories
every file. Its report-level \code{replay.py} runs only an explicit
allowlist of inspected, independently authored bounded checkers.
Upstream repository Python, compiler recipes, and saved arithmetic
source rows are never imported, evaluated, or executed. Their
\code{.py} and JSON files are hashed or parsed strictly as data.

One independent audit checker contains a historical absolute source
root. The wrapper verifies that exact line and its frozen file hash,
copies the checker to a temporary tree, and substitutes only that root
with the copied sibling Report45 packet path. It records both frozen
and executed-copy hashes. No scientific packet is modified. All
checker outputs are compared with their frozen receipts in normal and
\code{python -O} modes, and packet hashes are checked before and after.

The report can be replayed with \code{python3 replay.py}, rebuilt with
\code{python3 build_pdf.py --output /tmp/Report46-rebuilt.pdf}, and
checked with \code{python3 verify_release.py}. The build uses ordinary
local LaTeX with shell escape disabled; it never runs research code.
The bounded checkers require Python 3; the two independent analytic
checkers additionally use \code{mpmath 1.3.0}, as recorded in their
receipts. All other replay checkers use the standard library.
The supplied build receipts, replay receipt, and PDF-layout QA record
the observed outcomes. The release contains editable LaTeX as well as
the PDF. The ZIP manifest excludes itself and the ZIP to avoid a
self-hash cycle.

\subsection{What remains outside the conclusion}
\begin{enumerate}
\item The height is the exact height of this family and the exact
minimum among auxiliary completions with its outer tuple fixed.
It is not the globally smallest certificate of square/product82.
\item The input bounds preserve the genuine radix jumps. They do not
assert a single smooth leading constant in the ordinary input.
\item The free83 theorem requires the specified even-rank sector and
$P_5=1$. Its nonextension result is compatible with the separately
audited all-input collapse through odd prime ranks
\cite{fup:eh:primecollapse}; it cannot be promoted into generic soundness.
\item The author packet's old ``pending audit'' wording is preserved
as source history. The separate completed audit supplies the final
core verdict; no frozen source bytes were edited to update prose.
\item The forward series, algebraic cubic inverse, and Lagrange
analytic inverse algorithm are genuinely convergent in their stated
domains. The explicitly isolated exponential sector is the first one;
the Lagrange algorithm is not a fully sorted canonical inverse
transseries. No numerical interval certification is claimed.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Further research questions}
\label{fup:eh:sec:future}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Can a different genuine outer construction reduce the height
below this doubly exponential family, perhaps to singly exponential
height in a suitable input parameter? The fixed-outer minimum prevents
an auxiliary-only improvement but says nothing about that broader search.
\item Can sharper packing analysis quantify the height oscillations
inside each radix plateau and across the factor-five jumps without
discarding the compiler dependence?
\item Can the convergent Lagrange inverse be reorganized into a
canonical ordered expansion of all exponential sectors, with uniform
tail bounds after that regrouping? The analytic algorithm and its
geometric tail are established here; such a sector-by-sector
reorganization requires additional justification.
\item Beyond the known odd-prime-index construction
\cite{fup:eh:primecollapse}, which outer invariants characterize free83 completions?
The positive branch's opposite-parity obstruction suggests distinguishing
main-rank parity from the independent negative-branch condition on $R$.
\item Can the nonsquarefree-lattice argument extend beyond $P_5=1$
to classify the actual divisor budgets contributed by nonunit retained
factors? A proof must control those factors rather than import a unit
normalization from a different circuit.
\item Can the seven one-sided ledger entries be sharpened to exact
lengths while preserving all genuine compiler branches? This would
refine coordinate accounting, although it cannot change the exact
maximum already proved here.
\end{enumerate}
